Uniform regularity in the low Mach number and inviscid limits for the full Navier-Stokes system in domains with boundaries
Changzhen Sun

Abstract
. In the present work, motivated by the studies on the low Mach number limit problem, we establish uniform regularity estimates with respect to the Mach number for the non-isentropic compressible Navier-Stokes system in smooth domains with Navier-slip boundary conditions, in the general case of ill-prepared initial data. The thermal conduction is taken into account and the large variation of temperature is allowed. Moreover, the obtained regularity estimates are also uniform in the Reynolds number Re ∈ [ 1 , + ∞ ) , \text{Re}\in[1,+\infty), Péclet number Pe ∈ [ 1 , + ∞ ) , \text{Pe}\in[1,+\infty), provided | 1 Re − ι 0 Pe | ≲ 1 Pe 1 2 ​ 1 Re , \big|\frac{1}{\text{Re}}-\frac{\iota_{0}}{\text{Pe}}\big|\lesssim\frac{1}{\text{Pe}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{1}{\text{Re}}, where ι 0 \iota_{0} is a fixed constant independent of Mach number, Reynolds number and Péclet number. The large temperature variation as well as the interactions of two kinds of boundary layers are the main obstacles of the proof.



Keywords:
uniform regularity, low Mach number limit, fast oscillation, boundary layers

1.
Introduction

In this manuscript, we consider the following scaled non-isentropic compressible Navier-Stokes system:

(1.1)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho^{\varepsilon}+{\rm div\,}(\rho^{\varepsilon}u^{\varepsilon})=0,\\
\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\rho^{\varepsilon}u^{\varepsilon})+{\rm div\,}(\rho^{\varepsilon}u^{\varepsilon}\otimes u^{\varepsilon})+\frac{\nabla P^{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon^{2}}-\mu\,{\rm div\,}\mathcal{L}u^{\varepsilon}=0,\qquad\text{$(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega$}\\[5.0pt]
\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\rho^{\varepsilon}e^{\varepsilon})+{\rm div\,}(\rho^{\varepsilon}u^{\varepsilon}e^{\varepsilon})+P^{\varepsilon}{\rm div\,}u^{\varepsilon}=\kappa\Delta{\mathcal{T}}^{\varepsilon}+\mu\varepsilon^{2}{\mathcal{L}}u^{\varepsilon}\cdot\mathbb{S}u^{\varepsilon},\end{array}\right.\\

where
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}

is a smooth domain in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
\rho^{\varepsilon}(t,x),

u^{\varepsilon}(t,x)
and
\mathcal{T}^{\varepsilon}
are respectively the density, the velocity, the temperature
of the fluid. Representing the pressure and the total energy
respectively,
P^{\varepsilon},e^{\varepsilon}
are given functions of the density and the temperature. We assume that the fluid is polytropic, that is,

P^{\varepsilon}=R\rho^{\varepsilon}\mathcal{T}^{\varepsilon},\qquad e^{\varepsilon}=C_{v}\mathcal{T}^{\varepsilon},

where the two fixed positive constants
R,C_{v}
denote respectively the generic gas constant and the heat at constant volume. Moreover,
the viscous stress tensor takes the form:

\mathcal{L}u^{\varepsilon}=2\lambda_{1}\,\mathbb{S}u^{\varepsilon}+\lambda_{2}\,{\rm div\,}u^{\varepsilon}\text{Id},\quad\mathbb{S}u^{\varepsilon}=\frac{1}{2}(\nabla u^{\varepsilon}+\nabla^{t}u^{\varepsilon}),

where
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
are viscosity parameters that are assumed to be constant and to satisfy the physical condition:

\mu>0,\quad 2\mu+3\lambda>0.

Defined as the ratio of characteristic fluid velocity to the sound speed, the Mach number
\varepsilon
is a dimensionless parameter that meassures the compressibility of the fluid.
The parameters
\mu,\kappa\in(0,1]
are respectively the inverse of the Reynold number and Péclet number that characterize the effects of hydrodynamical dissipation and thermal dissipation. In the current work, they are assumed to be small:

\varepsilon\in(0,1],\quad\mu\in(0,1],\quad\kappa\in(0,1].

As we are considering the system in a domain with boundaries,
we shall supplement the system (
1.1
)
with the Navier-slip boundary condition on the velocity and the Neumann boundary condition on the temperature

(1.2)

u^{\varepsilon}\cdot\textbf{n}=0,\quad\Pi(\mathbb{S}u^{\varepsilon}\textbf{n})+a\Pi u^{\varepsilon}=0,\quad\partial_{\textbf{n}}{\mathcal{T}}^{\varepsilon}=0\quad\text{on }{\partial{\Omega}}

where
n
is the unit outward normal vector and
a
is a constant related to the slip length
(our analysis can be easily extended to the case where
a
is a smooth function). We use the notation
\Pi f
for the tangential part of a vector
f,

\Pi f^{\varepsilon}=f^{\varepsilon}-(f^{\varepsilon}\cdot\textbf{n})\cdot\textbf{n}.
Let us remark that Navier-slip boundary conditions
can be expressed as a non-homogeneous Dirichlet condition on
\mathop{\rm curl}\nolimits u^{\varepsilon}\times\textbf{n},

(1.3)

\mathop{\rm curl}\nolimits u^{\varepsilon}\times\textbf{n}=2\Pi(-au^{\varepsilon}+D\textbf{n}\cdot u^{\varepsilon})\quad\text{on }{\partial{\Omega}}.

The special case
\mathop{\rm curl}\nolimits u^{\varepsilon}\times\textbf{n}=0
corresponds to the choice
a=D\textbf{n}.

Taking the smallness of Mach number into account, the scaled system (
1.1
) can be derived from the original non-isentropic compressible Navier-Stokes system by introducing suitable change of variable. As a matter of fact, one finds (
1.1
) by performing the following scaling:

\rho(t,x)=\rho^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon t,x),\,u(t,x)=\varepsilon u^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon t,x),\,{\mathcal{T}}={\mathcal{T}}^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon t,x),\,\tilde{\mu}=\varepsilon\mu,\,\tilde{\kappa}=\varepsilon\kappa,

where
(\rho,u,{\mathcal{T}})
solve the following system:

(1.4)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho+{\rm div\,}(\rho u)=0,\\
\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\rho u)+{\rm div\,}(\rho u\otimes u)-\tilde{\mu}\,{\rm div\,}\mathcal{L}u+\nabla P=0,\qquad\text{$(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega$},\\
\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\rho e)+{\rm div\,}(\rho ue)+P{\rm div\,}u=\tilde{\kappa}\Delta{\mathcal{T}}+\tilde{\mu}{\mathcal{L}}u\cdot\mathbb{S}u,\end{array}\right.\\

with

P=R\rho\mathcal{T},\qquad e=C_{v}\mathcal{T}.

We are interested in the limit of the solution to (
1.1
) as
\varepsilon
tends to
0.
Since the pressure depends both on the density and the temperature, it is not very clear to see what should be the limit system as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0.
However, as noted in
[
2
]
, it is more convenient to review the system (
1.1
) as the equations of the pressure
P^{\varepsilon},
the velocity
u^{\varepsilon}
and the temperature
{\mathcal{T}}^{\varepsilon}

and thus to consider the following equivalent system:

(1.5)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+u^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla)P^{\varepsilon}+\gamma P^{\varepsilon}{\rm div\,}u^{\varepsilon}-(\gamma-1)\kappa\Delta{\mathcal{T}}^{\varepsilon}=(\gamma-1)\mu\varepsilon^{2}{\mathcal{L}}u^{\varepsilon}\cdot\mathbb{S}u^{\varepsilon},\\[5.0pt]
\displaystyle\rho^{\varepsilon}(\partial_{t}+u^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla)u^{\varepsilon}+\frac{\nabla P^{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon^{2}}-\mu\,{\rm div\,}\mathcal{L}u^{\varepsilon}=0,\qquad\text{$(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega$},\\[5.0pt]
\displaystyle\rho^{\varepsilon}C_{v}(\partial_{t}+u^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla){\mathcal{T}}^{\varepsilon}+P^{\varepsilon}{\rm div\,}u^{\varepsilon}=\kappa\Delta{\mathcal{T}}^{\varepsilon}+\mu\varepsilon^{2}{\mathcal{L}}u^{\varepsilon}\cdot\mathbb{S}u^{\varepsilon},\end{array}\right.\\

where
\gamma=1+C_{v}
denotes the ratio of specific heat. We can thus deduce formally that the limit system takes the form:

(1.6)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\gamma\overline{P}\,{\rm div\,}u^{0}=(\gamma-1)\kappa\Delta{\mathcal{T}}^{0},\\
\displaystyle\rho^{0}(\partial_{t}+u^{0}\cdot\nabla)u^{0}+\nabla\pi^{0}-\mu{\rm div\,}{\mathcal{L}}u^{0}=0,\\
\displaystyle C_{v}\gamma\rho^{0}(\partial_{t}+u^{0}\cdot\nabla){\mathcal{T}}^{0}=\kappa\Delta{\mathcal{T}}^{0},\end{array}\right.\\

where
\rho^{0}=\overline{P}/R{\mathcal{T}}^{0}.

Naturally, if the temperature
{\mathcal{T}}^{\varepsilon}
is assumed to be a constant
\overline{{\mathcal{T}}},
then the non-isentropic system (
1.5
) reduces to the isentropic system and the limit system (
1.6
) reduces to the incompressible Navier-Stokes system. This limit process is therefore frequently referred to as the incompressible limit when the isentropic fluids are considered.

The current work is motivated by the verification of this limit process for strong solutions to (
1.5
).
Partially due to the appearance of the
singular term
\frac{\nabla P^{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon^{2}}

in the system (
1.5
), this limit process is considered to be a singular limit. Since we are dealing with strong solutions, the first (and highly non-trivial) part is to establish some uniform estimates independent of the Mach number
\varepsilon,
so that the solutions to (
1.5
) for any
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
exist on a time interval independent of
\varepsilon.
Once this has been done, the convergence of solutions of (
1.5
) to
that of (
1.6
) will be shown when
\Omega
is an exterior domain outside a compact smooth domain in
\mathbb{R}^{3}.

Due to its great physical interests, the low Mach number limit problem has been investigated widely in several different contexts, depending on the type of solutions (strong solutions or weak solutions), the generality of the system (isentropic or non-isentropic), the properties of the domain (whole space, torus or domain with boundaries), as well as the type of the initial data considered.

We will first review some works concerning the incompressible limit for
isentropic
compressible system.
The initial works, credited to Ebin
[
14
]
, Klainerman-Majda
[
34
,
35
]
, focus on the study of the local strong solution of isentropic fluids occupied in a domain without boundaries (
\mathbb{R}^{3}
or
\mathbb{T}^{3}
), with well-prepared initial data (
({\rm div\,}u_{0}^{\varepsilon},\nabla P_{0}^{\varepsilon}/\varepsilon)={\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon)
). Later, the same problem is considered by Ukai
[
56
]
in the whole space and Gallagher
[
21
]
on the torus for ill-prepared initial data (
({\rm div\,}u_{0}^{\varepsilon},\nabla P_{0}^{\varepsilon}/\varepsilon)={\mathcal{O}}(1)
).
Recently, the author and his collaborators investigate in
[
43
]
the incompressible limit of the strong solution in domain with boundaries for ill-prepared data, by showing the uniform regularity estimates.
The incompressbile limit of weak solutions for the viscous system was first investigated by Lions and Masmoudi. In
[
36
,
37
]
, they prove that the weak solutions of the isentropic compressible Navier-Stokes system converge to the solution of the incompressible system. In general, for ill-prepared data, one can only obtain weak convergence in time, nevertheless, Desjardins and Grenier establish in
[
11
]
the local strong convergence by using the dispersion of acoustic wave in the whole space. There are also many other related works, one can see for instance
[
4
,
5
,
6
,
8
,
10
,
12
,
15
,
18
,
28
,
30
,
38
,
48
]
.
For more exhaustive information, one can refer to the well-written survey papers by
Alazard
[
3
]
, Danchin
[
9
]
, Feireisl
[
17
]
, Gallagher
[
22
]
,
Jiang-Masmoudi
[
30
]
,
Schochet
[
52
]
.

Let us focus on the low Mach number limit of the strong solution to non-isentropic system, which is more related to the interest of the current work. For the non-isentropic inviscid system (that is
\mu=\kappa=0
in system (
1.1
)), Metivier and Schochet
[
45
]
study the low Mach number limit of strong solution in the whole space
\mathbb{R}^{3}
for ill-prepared data. More precisely, they establish uniform estimates and justify the
the convergence of the solution of the non-isentopic Euler system to the solution of the incompressible inhomogeneous Euler system.
In a bounded domain, the local strong solution is established by Schochet
[
51
]
and the low Mach number limit is shown for the well-prepared data. As for the ill-prepared data, Alazard
[
1
]
establishes the uniform estimates in domains with boundaries. Moreover, for an exterior domain, he proves the similar convergence result as in
[
45
]
. With regards to the non-isentropic Navier-Stokes system (
1.5
), there are several works depending on the property of the domain and the type of the initial data. In the whole space,
Alazard proves
[
2
]
the uniform estimates and the low Mach number limit for
ill-prepared
data. It is worthy to mention that the estimates obtained in
[
2
]
are uniform not only in Mach number
\varepsilon
, but also in the Reynolds number
\text{Re}=\mu^{-1}
and the Péclet number
\text{Pe}=\kappa^{-1}
. We also refer to
[
31
]
for the results on the non-isentopic MHD system.
However, for the non-isentropic viscous system in a domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
with boundaries, there are only a few works and all of them deal with
well-prepared
data. In
[
50
]
, Jiang and Ou obtain uniform estimates
for (
1.1
) with Dirichlet boundary condition imposed on the velocity (
u^{\varepsilon}|_{\partial\Omega}=0
), for well-prepared data in the case of vanishing thermal conductivity (
\kappa=0
). The authors in
[
13
,
33
]
consider the low Mach number limit for (
1.5
) in the case of

\kappa>0
with the
vorticity-slip
boundary condition
\mathop{\rm curl}\nolimits u^{\varepsilon}\times\textbf{n}=0.
Under the well-prepared assumption they can establish uniform estimates in the Mach number and prove the convergence of
(
1.5
) to the isentropic incompressible Navier-Stokes system. Nevertheless, in the general case of ill-prepared initial data and Navier-slip boundary conditions (
1.3
),
the low Mach number limit for non-isentropic viscous system (
1.5
)
has not been addressed so far.

On the other hand, as in the concrete physics, the dimensionless physical quantities are likely to be related, it is interesting to obtain uniform regularity estimates in all of these parameters.
Therefore, in this work, we aim to establish uniform estimates not only in the Mach number, but also in the Reynolds number
\text{Re}=\mu^{-1}
and the Péclet number
\text{Pe}=\kappa^{-1}
(here the parameter
\kappa
appears since we consider non-isentropic system), which will be convenient to study simultaneously the low Mach number and the inviscid limit for the
strong solutions
.
As will be explained carefully in Subsection 1.3, there are several difficulties in order to obtain such a result.

The similar problem has been studied by Alazard
[
2
]
in the whole space.
As noted in
[
45
]
, the non-isentropic system is not uniformly linear stable due to the non-constant temperature, which prevents one from obtaining high regularity estimates by differentiating the equation. Alazard
[
2
]
can overcome this problem in the whole space by splitting the frequencies and by employing the para-differential calculus. Nevertheless, it is unclear how could
this strategy be applied
in the domain with boundaries. Moreover, in the presence of the boundaries,
due to the non-vanishing viscosity
\mu
and thermal conductivity
\kappa,
two different kinds of boundary layers, namely the viscous boundary layer (due to the non-zero viscosity) and the thermal boundary layer (due to the non-zero thermal conductivity) will show up and interact with each other. This phenomenon can make the analysis rather delicate and some suitable assumption between the thermal conductivity
\kappa
and the viscosity
\mu
has to be made.

Due to the appearance of the boundary layers,
to get uniform high order estimates, we need to use a functional framework based on
conormal Sobolev spaces that minimize the use of normal derivatives close to the boundary
in the spirit of
[
41
,
42
,
40
,
43
]
.
We remark that such kind of spaces has
been widely used to study initial boundary value problems for parabolic
and hyperbolic equations, see for example
[
53
]
,
[
27
,
55
]
,
[
24
,
44
,
47
]
.

1.1.
Conormal Sobolev spaces and notations

To define the conormal Sobolev norms, we take a finite set of generators of vector fields
that are tangent to the boundary of
\Omega
:
Z_{j}(1\leq j\leq M)
.
Due to the appearance of the fast oscillations, it is also necessary to involve the scaled time derivative

Z_{0}=\varepsilon\partial_{t}.

We set

Z^{I}=Z_{0}^{\alpha_{0}}\cdots Z_{M}^{{\alpha_{M}}},\quad I=(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\cdots\alpha_{M})\in\mathbb{N}^{M+1}.

Note that
Z^{I}
contains not only spatial derivatives but also the scaled time derivative
\varepsilon\partial_{t}.

We introduce the following Sobolev conormal spaces: for
p=2
or
+\infty,

L_{t}^{p}H_{co}^{m}=\{f\in L^{p}\big([0,t],L^{2}(\Omega)\big),Z^{I}f\in L^{p}\big([0,t],L^{2}(\Omega)\big),|I|\leq m\},

equipped with the norm:

(1.7)

\|f\|_{L_{t}^{p}H_{co}^{m}}=\sum_{|I|\leq m}\|Z^{I}f\|_{L^{p}([0,t],L^{2}(\Omega))},

where
|I|=\alpha_{0}+\cdots\alpha_{M}.

Since the number of time derivatives and spatial conormal derivatives need sometimes to be distinguished, we shall also use the notation:

(1.8)

\|f\|_{L_{t}^{p}\mathcal{H}^{j,l}}=\sum_{I=(k,\tilde{I}),k\leq j,|\tilde{I}|\leq l}\|Z^{I}f\|_{L^{p}([0,t],L^{2}(\Omega))}

and to simplify, we will use
L_{t}^{p}\mathcal{H}^{l}=L_{t}^{p}\mathcal{H}^{0,l}.

We will also use sometimes the following norm that preclude the highest time derivatives:

(1.9)

\|f\|_{L_{t}^{p}\underline{H}_{co}^{m}}=\sum_{j+l\leq m,j\leq m-1}\|f\|_{L_{t}^{p}\mathcal{H}^{j,l}}

Moreover, we introduce for convenience the notation:

\displaystyle\|f\|_{L_{t}^{p}H_{co,\nu}^{m}}=\nu\|f\|_{L_{t}^{p}H_{co}^{m}}+\|f\|_{L_{t}^{p}H_{co}^{m-1}}.

For the space modeled on
L^{\infty}
, we shall use the following notation:

(1.10)

\interleave f\interleave_{m,\infty,t}=\sum_{|I|\leq m}\|Z^{I}f\|_{L^{\infty}([0,t]\times\Omega)}.

To measure pointwise regularity at a given time
t
(in particular also with
t=0
), we shall use the semi-norms

(1.11)

\displaystyle\|f(t)\|_{H_{co}^{m}}=\sum_{|I|\leq m}

\displaystyle\|(Z^{I}f)(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)},\quad\|f(t)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{j,l}}=\sum_{I=(k,\tilde{I}),k\leq j,|\tilde{I}|\leq l}\|Z^{I}f(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)},

\displaystyle\|f(t)\|_{m,\infty}=\sum_{|I|\leq m}\|(Z^{I}f)(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}.

Finally, to measure regularity along the boundary, we use

(1.12)

|f|_{L_{t}^{p}\tilde{H}^{s}(\partial\Omega)}=\sum_{j=0}^{[s]}|(\varepsilon\partial_{t})^{j}f|_{L^{p}([0,t],{H}^{s-j}(\partial\Omega))}.

Let us recall, how the vector fields
Z_{j}
,
1\leq j\leq M
can be defined. We consider

\Omega\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
a smooth domain whose boundaries can be covered by a finite number of charts. More precisely, there exist sets
\Omega_{i},\,i=0,\cdots N
such that

(1.13)

\Omega\subset\Omega_{0}\cup_{i=1}^{N}\Omega_{i},\quad\Omega_{0}\Subset\Omega,\quad\Omega_{i}\cap\partial\Omega\neq\varnothing,

and
\Omega_{i}\cap\partial\Omega
is the graph of a smooth function
z=\varphi_{i}(x_{1},x_{2})
. Note that any smooth bounded domain or exterior domain outside a smooth compact set in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
admit such property.

In
\Omega_{0}
, we just take the vector fields
\partial_{k}
,
k=1,\,2,\,3
.
To define appropriate vector fields near the boundary, we use the local coordinates in each
\Omega_{i}:

(1.14)

\displaystyle\Phi_{i}:

\displaystyle(-\delta_{i},\delta_{i})^{2}\times(0,\epsilon_{i})\rightarrow\Omega_{i}\cap\Omega

\displaystyle(y,z)^{t}\rightarrow\Phi_{i}(y,z)=(y,\varphi_{i}(y)+z)^{t}

and we define the vector fields (up to some smooth cut-off functions compactly supported in
\Omega_{i}
) as :

(1.15)

Z_{k}^{i}=\partial_{y^{k}}=\partial_{k}+\partial_{k}\varphi_{i}\partial_{3},\quad k=1,2\qquad Z_{3}^{i}=\phi(z)(\partial_{1}\varphi_{1}\partial_{1}+\partial_{2}\varphi_{1}\partial_{2}-\partial_{3}),

where
\phi(z)=\frac{z}{1+z},
and
\partial_{k},k=1,2,3
are the derivations with respect to the original coordinates of
\mathbb{R}^{3}.
We remark that if
\Omega=\mathbb{R}_{+}^{3},
the conormal vector fields can be defined globally due to the flat boundary:

Z_{1}=\partial_{1},\,Z_{2}=\partial_{2},\,Z_{3}=\frac{z}{1+z}\partial_{z}.

We shall denote by
n
the unit outward normal to the the boundary.
In each
\Omega_{i}
, we can extend it to
\Omega_{i}
by setting

\textbf{n}(\Phi_{i}(y,z))=\frac{1}{|\textbf{N}|}\textbf{N},\quad\textbf{N}(\Phi_{i}(y,z))=(\partial_{1}\varphi_{i}(y),\partial_{2}\varphi_{i}(y),-1)^{t}.

In the same way, the projection on vector fields tangent to the boundary,

\Pi=\text{Id}-\textbf{n}\otimes\textbf{n}

can be extended in
\Omega_{i}
by using the extension of
n
.

Let us observe that by identity

\Pi(\partial_{\textbf{n}}u^{\varepsilon})=\Pi((\nabla u^{\varepsilon})\textbf{n})=2\Pi(\mathbb{S}u^{\varepsilon})-\Pi((Du^{\varepsilon})\textbf{n})

with
[(\nabla u^{\varepsilon})\textbf{n}]_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{3}\textbf{n}_{k}\partial_{k}u_{i}^{\varepsilon},[(Du^{\varepsilon})\textbf{n}]_{i}=\sum_{k=1}^{3}\partial_{i}u_{k}^{\varepsilon}\textbf{n}_{k},

the boundary conditions
(
1.2
)
can be reformulated as:

(1.16)

u^{\varepsilon}\cdot\textbf{n}=0,\quad\Pi(\partial_{\textbf{n}}u^{\varepsilon})=\Pi[-2au^{\varepsilon}+(D\textbf{n})u^{\varepsilon}],\quad\partial_{\textbf{n}}\theta^{\varepsilon}=0\text{ on }{\partial\Omega},

where
[(D\textbf{n})u^{\varepsilon}]_{i}=\sum_{k=1}^{3}\partial_{i}\textbf{n}_{k}u_{k}^{\varepsilon}.

1.2.
Main results

Since the pressure
P^{\epsilon},
the temperature
{\mathcal{T}}^{\varepsilon}
are strictly positive, we denote

\sigma^{\varepsilon}=\colon\frac{\ln P^{\varepsilon}-\ln\overline{P}}{\varepsilon},\quad\theta^{\varepsilon}=\colon\ln{\mathcal{T}}^{\varepsilon}-\ln\overline{{\mathcal{T}}}

or equivalently,

P^{\varepsilon}=\overline{P}e^{\varepsilon\sigma^{\varepsilon}},\quad{\mathcal{T}}^{\varepsilon}=\overline{{\mathcal{T}}}e^{\theta^{\varepsilon}}

where
\overline{P},\overline{{\mathcal{T}}}
are two reference constants.
The system (
1.5
) can then be written in the following way:

(1.17)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\frac{1}{\gamma}(\partial_{t}+u^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla)\sigma^{\varepsilon}+\frac{{\rm div\,}u^{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon}-\frac{\gamma-1}{\gamma\varepsilon}\kappa\Gamma(\varepsilon\sigma^{\varepsilon}){\rm div\,}(\beta(\theta^{\varepsilon})\nabla\theta^{\varepsilon})=\frac{\gamma-1}{\gamma}\mathfrak{N}^{\varepsilon},\\[5.0pt]
\displaystyle\frac{1}{R\beta(\theta^{\varepsilon})}(\partial_{t}+u^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla)u^{\varepsilon}+\frac{\nabla\sigma^{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon}-\mu\Gamma(\varepsilon\sigma^{\varepsilon})\,{\rm div\,}\mathcal{L}u^{\varepsilon}=0,\qquad\text{$(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega$},\\[5.0pt]
\displaystyle\frac{C_{v}}{R}(\partial_{t}+u^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla)\theta^{\varepsilon}+{\rm div\,}u^{\varepsilon}-\kappa\Gamma(\varepsilon\sigma^{\varepsilon}){\rm div\,}(\beta(\theta^{\varepsilon})\nabla\theta^{\varepsilon})=\varepsilon\mathfrak{N}^{\varepsilon},\end{array}\right.\\

where we denote:

(1.18)

\Gamma(\varepsilon\sigma^{\varepsilon})=\frac{1}{P^{\varepsilon}}(\varepsilon\sigma^{\varepsilon}),\quad\beta(\theta^{\varepsilon})={\mathcal{T}}^{\varepsilon}(\theta^{\varepsilon}),\quad\mathfrak{N}^{\varepsilon}=\mu\varepsilon\Gamma(\varepsilon\sigma^{\varepsilon}){\mathcal{L}}u^{\varepsilon}\cdot\mathbb{S}u^{\varepsilon}.

From now on, we will work on the above system.

In the study of the strong solution of the above hyperbolic-parabolic system with parameters
(\varepsilon,\mu,\kappa)
small, there shall be two kinds of boundary layers-the viscous boundary layer with width
(\varepsilon\mu)^{\frac{1}{2}}
(incorporated with the fast oscillation effects) due to the non-zero viscous term in the equation of the velocity
u^{\varepsilon},

the thermal boundary layer with width
\kappa^{\frac{1}{2}}
due to the non-vanishing thermal conductivity in the equation of
\theta^{\varepsilon}.
The interactions of these two kinds of boundary layers can be quite involved, which may indeed prevent one from establishing uniform in
(\varepsilon,\mu,\kappa)
estimates for (
1.17
).
However, we are able to construct such uniform estimates provided the following assumption on
\mu
and
\kappa
holds:

(1.19)

\displaystyle\big|\mu-\frac{\kappa}{C_{v}\gamma\lambda_{1}}\big|\lesssim\mu\kappa^{\frac{1}{2}}.

We will explain why this assumption needs to be imposed in the next subsection.

In order to establish uniform high regularity estimates,
we shall use the following quantity:

(1.20)

\mathcal{N}^{\mu,\kappa}_{m,T}(\sigma^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon},\theta^{\varepsilon})=\mathcal{E}_{m,T}^{\mu,\kappa}(\sigma^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon},\theta^{\varepsilon})+\mathcal{A}_{m,T}^{\mu,\kappa}(\sigma^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon},\theta^{\varepsilon})

where

(1.21)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\mu,\kappa}_{m,T}(\sigma^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon},\theta^{\varepsilon})=\mathcal{E}^{\mu,\kappa}_{m,T}(\sigma^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon})+\mathcal{E}_{m,T}^{\kappa}(\theta^{\varepsilon}),\,\,\,\mathcal{A}^{\mu,\kappa}_{m,T}(\sigma^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon},\theta^{\varepsilon})=\mathcal{A}_{m,T}^{\mu,\kappa}(\sigma^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon})+\mathcal{A}^{\kappa}_{m,T}(\theta^{\varepsilon}),

with

(1.22)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\mu,\kappa}_{m,T}(\sigma^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle=\colon\varepsilon\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}\big(\|(\sigma^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon})\|_{L_{T}^{\infty}\underline{H}_{co}^{m}}+\|\nabla(\sigma^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon})\|_{L_{T}^{\infty}H_{co}^{m-1}}+\kappa^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\nabla u^{\varepsilon}\|_{L_{T}^{2}\underline{H}_{co}^{m}}\big)

\displaystyle+\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}\mu\|\nabla^{2}u^{\varepsilon}\|_{L_{T}^{2}H_{co,\sqrt{\varepsilon}}^{m-1}}+\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{3}}\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\nabla^{2}u^{\varepsilon}\|_{L_{T}^{2}H_{co}^{m-2}}+\varepsilon\mu\|\nabla^{2}(\sigma^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon})\|_{L_{T}^{\infty}H_{co}^{m-2}}

\displaystyle+\|(\sigma^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon})\|_{L_{T}^{\infty}H_{co}^{m-1}}+\|\nabla(\sigma^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon})\|_{L_{T}^{\infty}H_{co}^{m-2}}+\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\nabla(\sigma^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon})\|_{L_{T}^{2}H_{co}^{m-1}}

\displaystyle+\kappa^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\nabla(\sigma^{\varepsilon},{\rm div\,}u^{\varepsilon})\|_{L_{T}^{2}H_{co}^{m-2}}+\|\nabla^{2}\sigma^{\varepsilon}\|_{L_{T}^{\infty}H_{co}^{m-3}},

(1.23)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{m,T}^{\kappa}(\theta^{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle=\kappa^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\theta^{\varepsilon}\|_{L_{T}^{\infty}\underline{H}_{co}^{m}}+\|\theta^{\varepsilon}\|_{L_{T}^{\infty}{H}_{co}^{m-1}}+\|\nabla\theta^{\varepsilon}\|_{L_{T}^{\infty}H_{co,\sqrt{\kappa}}^{m-1}}

\displaystyle+\kappa^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\nabla^{2}\theta^{\varepsilon}\|_{L_{T}^{\infty}H_{co,\sqrt{\kappa}}^{m-2}\cap L_{T}^{2}H_{co,\sqrt{\kappa}}^{m-1}}+\kappa\|\nabla^{3}\theta^{\varepsilon}\|_{L_{T}^{2}H_{co,\sqrt{\kappa}}^{m-2}},

(1.24)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{m,T}^{\mu,\kappa}(\sigma^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon})=\colon|\!|\!|(\sigma^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon})|\!|\!|_{m-3,\infty,T}+|\!|\!|(\nabla\sigma^{\varepsilon},(\varepsilon\mu)^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla u^{\varepsilon})|\!|\!|_{m-4,\infty,T}

\displaystyle+|\!|\!|(\nabla u^{\varepsilon},\varepsilon\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla^{2}(\sigma^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon}))|\!|\!|_{m-5,\infty,T},

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{m,T}^{\kappa}(\theta^{\varepsilon})=\colon|\!|\!|(\theta^{\varepsilon},\kappa\nabla\theta^{\varepsilon})|\!|\!|_{m-3,\infty,T}+\kappa^{\frac{1}{2}}|\!|\!|\nabla\theta^{\varepsilon}|\!|\!|_{m-4,\infty,T}

\displaystyle+|\!|\!|(\nabla\theta^{\varepsilon},\kappa\nabla^{2}\theta^{\varepsilon})|\!|\!|_{m-5,\infty,T}+\kappa^{\frac{1}{2}}|\!|\!|\nabla^{2}\theta^{\varepsilon}|\!|\!|_{m-6,\infty,T}.

Note that the norms involved in the above definitions are defined in (
1.7
)-(
1.10
).

Definition 1

(Compatibility condition)
.

We say that
(\sigma_{0}^{\varepsilon},u_{0}^{\varepsilon},\theta_{0}^{\varepsilon})
satisfy the compatibility conditions up to order
m
if for any
j=0,1\cdots m-1,

(\varepsilon\partial_{t})^{j}(u^{\varepsilon}\cdot\textbf{n})\big|_{t=0}=(\varepsilon\partial_{t})^{j}(\partial_{\textbf{n}}\theta^{\varepsilon})|_{t=0}=0,\quad\Pi\big[\mathbb{S}\big((\varepsilon\partial_{t})^{j}u^{\varepsilon}|_{t=0}\big)\textbf{n}\big]=-a\Pi\big[(\varepsilon\partial_{t})^{j}u^{\varepsilon}|_{t=0}\big]\,\text{ on }\,\partial\Omega.\,

Note that the restriction of the time derivatives of the solution at the initial time can be expressed inductively by using the equations. For example, we have

(\varepsilon\partial_{t}u^{\varepsilon})(0)=R\beta(\theta^{\varepsilon}_{0})\big(\varepsilon\mu\Gamma(\varepsilon\sigma_{0}^{\varepsilon}){\rm div\,}\mathcal{L}u_{0}^{\varepsilon}-\nabla\sigma_{0}^{\varepsilon}\big)-\varepsilon u_{0}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla u_{0}^{\varepsilon}.

We thus define the admissible space for the initial data as

\displaystyle Y_{m}=\bigg\{(\sigma^{\varepsilon}_{0},u_{0}^{\varepsilon},\theta^{\varepsilon}_{0})\big|Y_{m,\mu,\kappa}^{\varepsilon}(0)<+\infty,(\sigma_{0}^{\varepsilon},u_{0}^{\varepsilon},\theta_{0}^{\varepsilon})\text{ satisfy the compatibility conditions up to order $m$}\bigg\}

where

(1.25)

\displaystyle Y_{m,\mu,\kappa}^{\varepsilon}(0)

\displaystyle=:\|\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}(\varepsilon\sigma^{\varepsilon},\varepsilon u^{\varepsilon}),\kappa^{\frac{1}{2}}\theta^{\varepsilon})(0)\|_{\underline{H}_{co}^{m}}+\|\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla(\varepsilon\sigma^{\varepsilon},\varepsilon u^{\varepsilon}),\kappa^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla\theta^{\varepsilon})(0)\|_{{H}_{co}^{m-1}}

\displaystyle+\|(\sigma^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon},\theta^{\varepsilon})(0)\|_{H^{m-1}_{co}}+\|\big(\nabla(\sigma^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon},\theta^{\varepsilon}),\partial_{t}\theta^{\varepsilon}\big)(0)\|_{H^{m-2}_{co}}+\|\varepsilon\mu\nabla^{2}\sigma^{\varepsilon}(0)\|_{H_{co}^{m-2}}

\displaystyle+\|\big(\nabla(\theta^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon}),\kappa^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla^{2}\theta^{\varepsilon},\varepsilon\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla^{2}(\sigma^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon})\big)(0)\|_{m-5,\infty}.

We refer to (
1.11
) for the definition of the above norms. Note that by using inductively the equations to express
the time derivatives,
Y_{m,\mu,\kappa}^{\varepsilon}(0)
can indeed be expressed in terms of the initial data.

The main result of this work is the following uniform estimates:

Theorem 1.1

(Uniform estimates )
.

Given an integer
m\geq 7
and a
C^{m+2}
smooth domain
\Omega
which admits the property (
1.13
).
Define the set

(1.26)

\displaystyle A=\big\{(\mu,\kappa)\in(0,1]^{2}\big|\,\mu,\kappa\textnormal{ satisfy the relation \eqref{assumption-mukpa}}\big\}.

Consider a family of initial data depending only on
\varepsilon,
such that
(\sigma_{0}^{\varepsilon},u_{0}^{\varepsilon},\theta_{0}^{\varepsilon})\in Y_{m},\,u_{0}^{\varepsilon}|_{\partial\Omega}=0

and

(1.27)

\displaystyle\sup_{(\mu,\kappa)\in A,\varepsilon\in(0,1]}Y_{m,\mu,\kappa}^{\varepsilon}(0)<+\infty.

There exist
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,1]
and
T_{0}>0,
such that, for any
0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
, any
(\mu,\kappa)\in A,
the
system (
1.17
), (
1.16
) has a unique solution
(\sigma^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon},\theta^{\varepsilon})

which satisfies:

(1.28)

\sup_{(\mu,\kappa)\in A,\varepsilon\in(0,1]}\mathcal{N}_{m,T_{0}}^{\mu,\kappa}(\sigma^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon},\theta^{\varepsilon})<+\infty,

where
\mathcal{N}_{m,T_{0}}^{\mu,\kappa}(\sigma^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon},\theta^{\varepsilon})
is defined in (
1.20
).

Remark 1.2
.

Since our main focus is on the low Mach number limit, we only keep the dependence of the solutions on the Mach number
\varepsilon,
but when
\mu,\kappa
vary, they do depend on
(\mu,\kappa).

Remark 1.3
.

In view of (
1.28
),
(
1.21
) and the third line of (
1.22
),
we have the following uniform in
\varepsilon
estimates for

(\sigma^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon}):

(1.29)

\displaystyle\|(\sigma^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon})\|_{L_{T_{0}}^{\infty}H_{co}^{m-1}}+\|\nabla(\sigma^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon})\|_{L_{T_{0}}^{2}H_{co,\sqrt{\mu}}^{m-1}}\lesssim{\mathcal{E}}_{m,{T_{0}}}^{\mu,\kappa}(\sigma^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon})<+\infty.

Due to the bad commutation properties of the space conormal derivatives with the singular part of the system, we can only control
(\sigma^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon})
uniformly (in
\varepsilon,\mu,\kappa
) to the regularity
m-1.
Moreover, due to the dissipation effects, we can also control the highest regularity in
L_{t}^{2}
type norm with a weight
\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Remark 1.4
.

In light of (
1.23
), we have the following uniform in
(\varepsilon,\mu,\kappa)
estimates for
\theta^{\varepsilon}
in the
m-1
regularity

(1.30)

\displaystyle\|\theta^{\varepsilon}\|_{L_{T_{0}}^{\infty}H_{co}^{m-1}}+\|(\nabla,\partial_{t})\theta^{\varepsilon}\|_{L_{T_{0}}^{\infty}H_{co}^{m-2}}\lesssim{\mathcal{E}}_{m,{T_{0}}}^{\kappa}(\theta^{\varepsilon}),

and
\kappa-
dependent estimates in highest regularity:

\displaystyle\kappa^{\frac{1}{2}}\big(\|\theta^{\varepsilon}\|_{L_{T_{0}}^{\infty}\underline{H}_{co}^{m}}+\|\nabla\theta^{\varepsilon}\|_{L_{T_{0}}^{\infty}H_{co}^{m-1}}\big)\lesssim{\mathcal{E}}_{m,{T_{0}}}^{\kappa}(\theta^{\varepsilon}).

This is because that,
\theta^{\varepsilon}
solves
\eqref{NCNS-S2}_{3}
-the convection-diffusion system, transported by the velocity
u^{\varepsilon},
which admits the property (
1.29
).

Remark 1.5
.

By the assumption (
1.19
) on
\mu,\kappa,
we can find a constant
C_{0}>1,
such that

\frac{1}{C_{0}}\mu\leq\kappa\leq C_{0}\mu
which means that
\kappa
and
\mu
are proportional.
The more restrictive assumption
(
1.19
)
is made to control the
L_{t}^{\infty}H_{co}^{m-2}
norm of
\nabla u^{\varepsilon},
see Remark
8.15
. Nevertheless, let us comment that, in the above result, the Mach number
\varepsilon
and the Reynolds number
\mu^{-1}
can be completely independent.

Remark 1.6
.

One may expect that when
\mu,\varepsilon>0,\kappa=0,
the uniform estimates in
(\varepsilon,\mu)
would be much easier to obtain due to the lack of thermal boundary layer.
It turns out not to be the case. Indeed, when
\kappa=0,
as the temperature is freely transported by the velocity
u^{\varepsilon},
its second normal derivative has a size

(\varepsilon\mu)^{-\frac{1}{2}}.
This would be a crucial issue when proving the uniform estimates for the (modified) vorticity which solves a transport-diffusion equation with a source term involving second normal derivative of
\theta^{\varepsilon}.

Remark 1.7
.

Based on the uniform estimate (
1.28
), if in addition, the temperature admits small variation or in other words
\theta^{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon\theta_{1}^{\varepsilon},
where

\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1]}\big(\|\theta_{1}^{\varepsilon}|_{t=0}\|_{H_{co}^{m-1}(\Omega)}+\|\nabla\theta_{1}^{\varepsilon}|_{t=0}\|_{H_{co}^{m-2}(\Omega)\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\big)<+\infty,

then by following the calculations in Section 4 and Section 8, this property can be propagated, namely:

(1.31)

\displaystyle\sup_{(\mu,\kappa)\in A}\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]}\big(\|\theta_{1}\|_{L_{T_{0}}^{\infty}H_{co}^{m-1}}+\|\nabla\theta_{1}\|_{L_{T_{0}}^{\infty}H_{co}^{m-2}\cap L^{\infty}([0,T_{0}]\times\Omega)}\big)<+\infty.

Remark 1.8
.

In order to prove uniform estimates in
\mu,\kappa
, it is assumed, due to some technical reasons (explained at the end of Subsection
1.3.3
), in Theorem
1.1
that the initial velocity vanishes on the boundary. However, it is somehow not a very stringent assumption in the context of the low Mach number limit problem. Indeed,
the compatibility conditions together with the assumption (
1.27
)
imply that the data are prepared (in the sense that the data can be small compared to
\varepsilon
) on the boundary. Nevertheless, this assumption can be dropped if one only proves uniform estimates in the Mach number
\varepsilon
, see the following theorem.

Following the similar (and easier) arguments as in the proof of Theorem
1.1
, we have also:

Theorem 1.9

(Uniform estimates fixed
(\mu,\kappa)
)
.

Assume that
\Omega
is a
C^{m+2}(m\geq 7)
smooth domain that admits the property (
1.13
).
Let
\mu,\kappa\in(0,1]^{2}
be fixed and
suppose the initial data
(\sigma_{0}^{\varepsilon},u_{0}^{\varepsilon},\theta_{0}^{\varepsilon})\in Y_{m}
satisfying

\displaystyle\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1]}Y_{m,\mu,\kappa}^{\varepsilon}(0)<+\infty,

then the system (
1.17
), (
1.16
) admits a unique solution
(\sigma^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon},\theta^{\varepsilon})
which enjoys the uniform in
\varepsilon
estimate:

(1.32)

\displaystyle\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{1}]}\mathcal{N}_{m,T_{1}}^{\mu,\kappa}(\sigma^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon},\theta^{\varepsilon})<_{\mu,\kappa}+\infty.

To prove Theorem
1.1
, the crucial step is to show the following uniform a priori estimate
which is the
heart
of this paper:

Proposition 1.10
.

Assume that
(\sigma_{0}^{\varepsilon},u_{0}^{\varepsilon},\theta_{0}^{\varepsilon})
satisfy the same assumptions as in Theorem
1.1
.
Let
\bar{c}>0
be a constant such that:

-{\bar{c}}\leq\varepsilon\sigma_{0}^{\varepsilon}(x),\,\theta_{0}^{\varepsilon}(x)\leq\bar{c},\quad\forall x\in\Omega,\varepsilon\in(0,1].

Denote
r_{0}(s_{1},s_{2})=\frac{1}{R\beta(s_{1})}\exp({\frac{Rs_{2}}{C_{v}\gamma}}).

Let
c_{0}\in(0,1]
be such that:

(1.33)

\displaystyle\forall s\in\big[-3{\bar{c}},3\bar{c}\big],

\displaystyle c_{0}\leq\Gamma(s),\beta(s)\leq 1/{c_{0}},\,\quad|(\Gamma,\beta)|_{C^{m}\big(\big[-3{\bar{c}},3\bar{c}\big]\big)}\leq{1}/{c_{0}},

\displaystyle\forall\,(s_{1},s_{2})\in\big[-3{\bar{c}},3\bar{c}\big]^{2},\,\,c_{0}\leq r_{0}(s_{1},s_{2})\leq{1}/{c_{0}}.

Assume that for some
T\in(0,1]
the following assumption holds:

(1.34)

-3{\bar{c}}\leq\varepsilon\sigma^{\varepsilon}(t,x),\varepsilon\tilde{\sigma}^{\varepsilon}(t,x),\theta^{\varepsilon}(t,x)\leq 3\bar{c},\quad\forall(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\Omega,\forall\varepsilon\in[0,1],

where
\tilde{\sigma}^{\varepsilon}=\sigma^{\varepsilon}-\varepsilon\mu(2\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}){\Gamma}(\varepsilon\sigma^{\varepsilon}){\rm div\,}u^{\varepsilon}.

Then, there exist
two polynomials
\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1}
(whose coefficients are independent of
\varepsilon,\mu,\kappa
), and a constant
\vartheta_{0}>0,

such that, for any
\varepsilon\in(0,1],(\mu,\kappa)\in A,
we have for a smooth enough solution of (
1.17
) on
[0,T]

the following estimate:

(1.35)

\mathcal{N}_{m,T}^{\mu,\kappa}(\sigma^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon},\theta^{\varepsilon})\leq\Lambda_{0}\big(\frac{1}{c_{0}},Y_{m,\mu,\kappa}^{\varepsilon}(0)\big)+(T+\varepsilon)^{\vartheta_{0}}\Lambda_{1}\big(\frac{1}{c_{0}},\mathcal{N}_{m,T}^{\mu,\kappa}(\sigma^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon},\theta^{\varepsilon})\big),

where
Y_{m,\mu,\kappa}^{\varepsilon}(0),\,\mathcal{N}^{\mu,\kappa}_{m,T}(\sigma^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon},\theta^{\varepsilon})
are defined in (
1.25
) (
1.20
) respectively.

Based on the uniform estimates established above, it is possible to have some convergence results in the low Mach number and inviscid limits.

In light of the uniform estimate (
1.30
),
up to extraction of subsequences,
\theta^{\varepsilon}
converges in
C([0,T_{0}],H_{loc}^{1}(\Omega))
to some
\theta^{0}
as
\varepsilon
tends to
0.
However, due to the lack of time compactness, one can only conclude from (
1.29
) that

(\sigma^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon})
converge in
L_{w}^{2}([0,T_{0}],L_{loc}^{2}(\Omega))
to some
(\sigma^{0},u^{0}).
When
\Omega
is a bounded domain, the weak in time convergence for
(\sigma^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon})

cannot be in general
improved, due to the
lack of large time dispersion and the interactions of fast oscillating acoustic waves with the physical boundaries. This will prevent one from justifying the limits

(u^{0},\theta^{0})
to be the solutions of the following limit system which is equivalent to (
1.6
):

(1.36)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\gamma\overline{P}\,{\rm div\,}u^{0}=(\gamma-1)\kappa{\rm div\,}(\beta(\theta^{0})\nabla\theta^{0}),\\
\displaystyle\frac{\overline{P}}{R\beta(\theta^{0})}(\partial_{t}+u^{0}\cdot\nabla)u^{0}+\nabla\pi^{0}+\mu\lambda_{1}\mathop{\rm curl}\nolimits\mathop{\rm curl}\nolimits u^{0}=0,\\
\displaystyle\frac{C_{v}}{R}\gamma\overline{P}(\partial_{t}+u^{0}\cdot\nabla)\theta^{0}=\kappa{\rm div\,}(\beta(\theta^{0})\nabla\theta^{0}),\end{array}\right.\\

supplemented with the boundary condition

(1.37)

\displaystyle u^{0}\cdot\textbf{n}|_{\partial\Omega}=\partial_{\textbf{n}}\theta^{0}|_{\partial\Omega}=0,\quad(\mathop{\rm curl}\nolimits u^{0}\times\textbf{n})|_{\partial\Omega}=2\Pi(-au^{0}+D\textbf{n}\cdot u^{0})|_{\partial\Omega}.

Nevertheless, when
\Omega
is an exterior domain,
due to the (global) dispersion property for the wave equation,
the strong convergence in time can be obtained by
proving local energy decay, either using the defect measure technique
[
1
]
or using the so-called RAGE theorem
[
15
,
20
]
.
Consequently, it is possible to show
the following convergence result.

Theorem 1.11

(Low mach number limit for fixed
\mu,\kappa
)
.

Assume that
\Omega
is the exterior of a compact smooth domain in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and
\mu,\kappa\in(0,1]
are fixed. Let
(\sigma_{0}^{\varepsilon},u_{0}^{\varepsilon},\theta_{0}^{\varepsilon})\in Y_{m}
and assume in addition:

(1.38)

\displaystyle\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1]}\|\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{t}\nabla\theta^{\varepsilon}(0)\|_{H_{co}^{1}}<+\infty.

Let
(\sigma^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon},\theta^{\varepsilon})\in C([0,T_{1}],L^{2}(\Omega))

be the solutions of the systems (
1.17
) (
1.16
) with fixed
\mu,\kappa\in(0,1]^{2}
satisfying the uniform (in
\varepsilon
) estimate
(
1.32
).
Assume further that

\theta^{\varepsilon}
converges in
C([0,T_{1}],H_{loc}^{1}(\Omega))\cap C([0,T_{1}]\times\Omega)
to
\theta^{0},
which admits the following decay property:

(1.39)

\displaystyle|\theta^{0}(t,x)|\leq A|x|^{-1-\delta},\quad|\nabla\theta^{0}(t,x)|\leq A|x|^{-2-\delta},\quad\,\forall\,(t,x)\in[0,T_{1}]\times\Omega.

Then
\sigma^{\varepsilon}\rightarrow 0,\,{\rm div\,}u^{\varepsilon}-\frac{\gamma-1}{\gamma}\kappa\Gamma(\varepsilon\sigma^{\varepsilon}){\rm div\,}(\beta(\theta^{\varepsilon})\nabla\theta^{\varepsilon})\rightarrow 0
in
L^{2}([0,T_{1}],L_{loc}^{2}(\Omega)).
Moreover,
u^{\varepsilon}
converges in
L^{2}([0,T_{1}],L_{loc}^{2}(\Omega))
to some
u^{0},
and

(u^{0},\theta^{0})
are the (unique) solutions to the system (
1.36
) that enjoys the additional regularity:

(1.40)

\displaystyle(u^{0},\theta^{0})\in L_{T_{1}}^{\infty}{\mathcal{H}}^{0,m-1},\quad(\nabla u^{0},\nabla\theta^{0},\partial_{t}\theta^{0})\in L_{T_{1}}^{\infty}{\mathcal{H}}^{0,m-2}\cap L^{\infty}([0,T_{1}]\times\Omega).

Remark 1.12
.

Due to the lack of control of the second normal derivatives of the limit velocity
u^{0},

the solution to the second equations in (
1.36
) should be interpreted in the weak sense:
for any
\psi\in C^{\infty}([0,T_{1}],C_{c}^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega}))
with

\psi\cdot\textbf{n}|_{\partial\Omega}=0,
the following identity holds: for every
0<t\leq T_{1},

(1.41)

\displaystyle\frac{\overline{P}}{R}\int_{\Omega}\big(\frac{u^{0}}{\beta(\theta^{0})}\cdot\psi\big)(t,\cdot)\ \mathrm{d}x+\mu\lambda_{1}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\mathop{\rm curl}\nolimits u^{0}\times\mathop{\rm curl}\nolimits\psi\ \mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\frac{\overline{P}}{R}\int_{\Omega}\big(\frac{u^{0}}{\beta(\theta^{0})}\cdot\psi\big)(0,\cdot)\ \mathrm{d}x-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\bigg(\frac{\overline{P}}{R}\frac{u^{0}}{\beta(\theta^{0})}\cdot\nabla u^{0}+\nabla\pi^{0}\bigg)\cdot\psi\ \mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle+\frac{\overline{P}}{R}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{0}}{\beta(\theta^{0})}\cdot\partial_{t}\psi+\partial_{t}\big(\frac{1}{\beta(\theta^{0})}\big)u^{0}\cdot\psi\ \mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}s+\mu\lambda_{1}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\partial\Omega}2\Pi(-au^{0}+D\textbf{n}\cdot u^{0})\cdot\psi\ \mathrm{d}S_{y}\mathrm{d}s.

The proof of the above theorem will be presented in Section 14.

Granted by our uniform estimates in Theorem
1.1
,
it is also interesting to study the low Mach number and the inviscid limit in the same time. More precisely, by assuming
\mu=\frac{\kappa}{C_{v}\gamma\lambda_{1}}=\varepsilon^{\alpha},\alpha>0
one expects to show that when
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0,

(\theta^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon})
will converge to the solution of the following inhomogeneous incompressible Euler system:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\frac{C_{v}}{R}\gamma\overline{P}(\partial_{t}+u^{0}\cdot\nabla)\theta^{0}=0,\\
\displaystyle\frac{\overline{P}}{R\beta(\theta^{0})}(\partial_{t}+u^{0}\cdot\nabla)u^{0}+\nabla\pi^{0}=0,\quad{\rm div\,}u^{0}=0.\end{array}\right.

Due to some technical reasons, it is difficult to prove such a result when the temperature admits large variation (in other words,
\theta^{\varepsilon}
is only bounded).
Nevertheless, in the spirit of Remark
1.7

and the Theorem 3.1 in
[
19
]
, the following convergence result holds:

Theorem 1.13
.

Let
\Omega
be the exterior of a compact smooth domain in
\mathbb{R}^{3}.
Let
\mu=\frac{\kappa}{C_{v}\gamma\lambda_{1}}=\varepsilon^{\alpha},0<\alpha<\frac{10}{3}.
Given
(\sigma_{0}^{\varepsilon},u_{0}^{\varepsilon},\theta_{0}^{\varepsilon})
satisfying the assumption stated in Theorem
1.1
and also the following:
u_{0}^{\varepsilon}\rightarrow u_{0}^{0}
in
(L^{2}(\Omega))^{3}
where
\mathbb{P}u_{0}^{0}\in(H^{s}(\Omega))^{3}
with

s>\frac{5}{2}.

Suppose further that

\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1]}\big(\|\theta_{0}^{\varepsilon}/\varepsilon\|_{H_{co}^{m-1}(\Omega)}+\|\nabla\theta_{0}^{\varepsilon}/\varepsilon\|_{H_{co}^{m-1}(\Omega)\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\big)<+\infty.

Then
(u^{\varepsilon},\theta^{\varepsilon}/\varepsilon)
converges in
L_{loc}^{\infty}\big((0,\tilde{T}_{0}],(L_{loc}^{2}(\Omega))^{3}\big)\times L_{loc}^{\infty}\big((0,\tilde{T}_{0}],L_{loc}^{q}(\Omega)\big),(1\leq q<2)

to
(v^{0},\theta_{1}^{0})
which are the solutions to the
following Euler-Boussinesq equations:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\frac{C_{v}}{R}\gamma\overline{P}(\partial_{t}+v^{0}\cdot\nabla)\theta_{1}^{0}=0,\\
\displaystyle\frac{\overline{P}}{R\beta(0)}(\partial_{t}+v^{0}\cdot\nabla)v^{0}+\nabla\pi^{0}=0,\quad{\rm div\,}v^{0}=0,\quad v^{0}|_{t=0}=\mathbb{P}u_{0}^{0},\end{array}\right.

where we denote
\tilde{T}_{0}=\min[T_{0},T_{max}]

where
T_{max}
is the maximal existence time of the solution to
\eqref{euler-bou}_{2}
in
H^{s}(\Omega).

The proof is based on the dispersive estimate which is admissible in the case of smooth exterior domain, together
with the relative entropy method which
has been successfully applied in many singular limit problem for the weak solutions to the viscous systems.
The above theorem has been shown in
[
19
]
in the framework of the weak solution, and thus can also be used for solutions enjoying our additional regularity, we will thus omit the proof.

1.3.
Difficulties and strategies

In this subsection, we explain the main difficulties in order to establish uniform in
(\varepsilon,\mu,\kappa)
estimate
(
1.35
). In what follows, we will skip the
\varepsilon
-dependence of the solution for notational clarity.

1.3.1.
Uniform estimates only in the Mach number.

Indeed, there have already been several difficulties when one tries to prove uniform only in Mach number
\varepsilon
estimates by letting
\mu
and
\kappa
fixed.

Compared to the non-isentropic system in the whole space
\mathbb{R}^{3},
some difficulties arise due to the presence of the boundaries. Indeed, the main feature here is the appearance of the boundary layers with fast time oscillations. This prevents us from applying the strategies employed in the vanishing viscosity problems

[
41
,
57
]
where the boundary layer occurs
without time oscillations. More precisely, in
[
41
,
57
]
, one controls the high order tangential derivatives by direct energy estimates, and then uses the vorticity to control the normal derivatives. Nevertheless, for the system with low Mach number, even the tangential derivative estimates are not easy to get, since the spatial tangential derivatives
do not commute with
\nabla,{\rm div\,},
defined with the standard derivations in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, and thus creates singular commutators. Without a priori knowledge on the tangential derivatives, the normal derivatives are hard to control. To get around this difficulty, our general strategy is to split the velocity into a compressible part and an incompressible part,
and control the former by the divergence of the velocity and the latter by direct energy estimates.
In this way, we can recover all the tangential spatial derivatives and thus control the normal derivatives by careful studies on the vorticity. Such a strategy has been employed in the previous work
[
43
]
where uniform in (and only in) Mach number regularity estimates are established for
isentropic
compressible Navier-Stokes system. However, as explained below,
some crucial modifications need to be made in the non-isentropic setting.

As mentioned above, one crucial step to control the spatial tangential derivatives is to get a uniform control of the incompressible part of the velocity. This requires
to apply the Leray projection (defined in (
6.3
)) on the equation
\eqref{NCNS-S2}_{2}
for the velocity
u
and to perform a direct energy estimates on the resultant equations:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{R\beta}(\partial_{t}+u\cdot\nabla)(\mathbb{P}u)+\nabla q-\mu\lambda_{1}\overline{\Gamma}\Delta(\mathbb{P}u)=-[\mathbb{P},\frac{1}{R\beta}]\partial_{t}u+\cdots

where
\cdots
denotes some terms that can be uniformly bounded in
L_{t}^{2}H_{co}^{m-1}.

Nevertheless, due to the non-constant temperature
\beta(\theta),
the commutator
[\mathbb{P},\frac{1}{R\beta}]\partial_{t}u
does not vanish and thus by the ill-prepared assumption (
\partial_{t}u|_{t=0}
is of order
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{-1})
), cannot be uniformly bounded in
\varepsilon.
Such a problem also happens when one studies the vorticity
\omega=\mathop{\rm curl}\nolimits u
which solves
the equations:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{R\beta}(\partial_{t}+u\cdot\nabla)\omega-\mu\lambda_{1}\overline{\Gamma}\Delta\omega=-\nabla\big(\frac{1}{R\beta}\big)\times\partial_{t}u+\cdots.

Therefore, instead of working on the incompressible part and the curl of the velocity
u
, we have to study these counterparts of the modified velocity
\frac{1}{R\beta(\theta)}u.
Their estimates would depend on the temperature in an essential way, which not only increase the computational complexity but also
in turn lead to some technical issues in order to prove uniform in Reynolds number and Pélect number estimates.
We shall explain the latter more carefully in the next subsection.

1.3.2.
New difficulties for the estimates uniform in Reynolds number and Péclet number.

As explained in the last section, we have to study the incompressible part of the quantity:
\frac{1}{R\beta}u,
which satisfies the equations:

(1.44)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+u\cdot\nabla)\big(\mathbb{P}(\frac{1}{R\beta}u)\big)+\nabla q-\mu\lambda_{1}\overline{\Gamma}\Delta\mathbb{P}\big(\frac{1}{R\beta}u\big)=(\frac{1}{R\beta})^{\prime}u(\partial_{t}+u\cdot\nabla)\theta+\cdots

In view of the definition (
1.21
),
to prove the uniform estimates (
1.35
), we need to control the
L_{t}^{\infty}H_{co}^{m-1}
norm of the above system, which is expected to be accomplished by direct energy estimate. However, by the equations

\eqref{NCNS-S2}_{3}
for
\theta,
we have that

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+u\cdot\nabla)\theta=\frac{R}{C_{v}}\big(-{\rm div\,}u+\kappa\Gamma(\varepsilon\sigma){\rm div\,}(\beta(\theta)\nabla\theta)+\varepsilon\mathfrak{N}\big),

which is not uniformly (in
\mu
) bounded in
L_{t}^{2}H_{co}^{m-1}.
Indeed, it turns out that one can only control
{\rm div\,}u
in
L_{t}^{2}H_{co}^{m-1}
with a weight
(\mu+\kappa)^{\frac{1}{2}}.
This prevents one from proving the estimates that are uniform in
\mu,\kappa.
The way to get over this problem is to introduce the unknown
r_{2}u
where

r_{2}=\frac{1}{R\beta(\theta)}\exp(\varepsilon R{\sigma}/C_{v}\gamma)=\frac{1}{R\beta(0)}\exp(-R\tilde{\theta}/{C_{v}}),\quad\tilde{\theta}=\frac{C_{v}\theta}{R}-\frac{\varepsilon\sigma}{\gamma},

and then study the incompressible part of this new unknown which solves:

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+u\cdot\nabla)\big(\mathbb{P}(r_{2}{u})\big)+\nabla q-\mu\lambda_{1}\overline{\Gamma}\Delta\big(\mathbb{P}(r_{2}{u})\big)=-\frac{R}{C_{v}}r_{2}u(\partial_{t}+u\cdot\nabla)\tilde{\theta}+\cdots.

Compared with (
1.44
),
the advantage of this equation is that now

(\partial_{t}+u\cdot\nabla)\tilde{\theta}=\gamma^{-1}(\kappa\Gamma{\rm div\,}(\beta\nabla\theta)+\varepsilon\mathfrak{N})

which can be uniformly bounded in
L_{t}^{2}H_{co}^{m-1}.

Once the (modified) incompressible part
\mathbb{P}(r_{2}u)
is controlled, the next
essential step in order to recover one normal derivative of the velocity is to study the modified vorticity
\mathop{\rm curl}\nolimits(r_{2}u)\times\textbf{n}=\colon\omega^{\textbf{n}}_{r_{2}},
where some other difficulties arise.
By straightforward computations, we find that
\omega^{\textbf{n}}_{r_{2}}
solves a transport-diffusion equation

\displaystyle\big(\partial_{t}+u\cdot\nabla-\mu\lambda_{1}\overline{\Gamma}r_{2}^{-1}\Delta\big)\omega^{\textbf{n}}_{r_{2}}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\rm curl}\nolimits\bigg(-\frac{R}{C_{v}}r_{2}u(\partial_{t}+u\cdot\nabla)\tilde{\theta}+\mu\lambda_{1}\overline{\Gamma}r_{2}^{-1}[\mathop{\rm curl}\nolimits\mathop{\rm curl}\nolimits,r_{2}]u\bigg)\times\textbf{n}+\cdots

(1.45)

\displaystyle=\colon\mathop{\rm curl}\nolimits G_{r_{2}}\times\textbf{n}+\cdots

with the non-homogeneous Dirichlet boundary condition:

(1.46)

\displaystyle\omega^{\textbf{n}}_{r_{2}}\big|_{\partial\Omega}=\frac{R\varepsilon}{C_{v}\gamma}r_{2}\partial_{\textbf{n}}\sigma\Pi u+2r_{2}\Pi\big(-au+(D\textbf{n})u\big)\big|_{\partial\Omega}.

Note that here and in what follows, we denote
\cdots
as the terms that can be easily controlled in the spaces we are interested in.
Due to non-homogeneous Dirichlet boundary condition, one cannot get uniform estimates for
\omega^{\textbf{n}}_{r_{2}}
by merely energy estimates.
In order to get the uniform estimate, we split
\omega^{\textbf{n}}_{r_{2}}
into two parts, one of which solves a (variable coefficient) heat equation with the nontrivial Dirichlet boundary condition and the vanishing source term and thus can be controlled by using the Green function.
The other satisfies a convection-diffusion equation with homogeneous Dirichlet boundary condition which is amenable to energy estimates. We comment that such an idea has been successfully applied in our previous works
[
40
,
43
]
for the uniform in Mach number estimates for
isentropic
system in domains with fixed or free boundaries. Nevertheless, as will be explained in the following, there are some significant differences for non-isentropic system and
extra difficulties
for uniform estimates in
\varepsilon,\mu,\kappa.

By applying the above strategy, we reduce the matter after the change of coordinates to the study of the following two systems in half space:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\big(\partial_{t}-\mu\lambda_{1}\overline{\Gamma}(\frac{{1}}{r_{2}})^{\Psi}\big|_{z=0}\partial_{z}^{2}\big)\zeta_{1}=0,\quad(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{3},\\[3.0pt]
\zeta_{1}|_{t=0}=0,\quad\zeta_{1}|_{z=0}=\omega^{\textbf{n}}_{r_{2}}|_{\partial\Omega}\circ\Psi,\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}(\partial_{t}+\tilde{u}\cdot\nabla){\zeta_{2}}-\mu\lambda_{1}\overline{\Gamma}(\frac{{1}}{r_{2}})^{\Psi}(\partial_{z}^{2}+\Delta_{{g}})\zeta_{2}={\mathcal{S}}_{2},\quad(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{3},\\[3.0pt]
\zeta_{2}|_{t=0}=\omega^{\textbf{n}}_{r_{2}}|_{t=0}\circ\Psi,\quad\zeta_{2}|_{z=0}=0,\end{array}\right.

where
\Psi
is the change of variable associated to the normal geodesic coordinates (
8.12
),
(\frac{{1}}{r_{0}})^{\Psi}=\frac{1}{r_{0}}\circ\Psi,\tilde{u}=(D\Psi)^{-\star}u,

\Delta_{g}
is defined in (
8.16
) and involves only tangential derivatives. Moreover,

{\mathcal{S}}_{2}=-(\tilde{u}\cdot\nabla){\zeta_{1}}+\mu\lambda_{1}\overline{\Gamma}\bigg(\big(\frac{1}{r_{0}}\big)^{\Psi}\Delta_{{g}}\zeta_{1}+\big(\big(\frac{1}{r_{0}}\big)^{\Psi}-\big(\frac{1}{r_{0}}\big)^{\Psi}\big|_{z=0}\big)\partial_{z}^{2}\zeta_{1}\bigg)+\big(\mathop{\rm curl}\nolimits G_{r_{2}}\times\textbf{n}\big)^{\Psi}+\cdots.

In light of the first term in
{\mathcal{S}}_{2},
to bound
\zeta_{2}
in the space
L_{t}^{\infty}H_{co}^{m-2},

we are obliged to control uniformly
\zeta_{1}
in
L_{t}^{2}H_{co}^{m-1}.
This in turn requires, the boundedness of
\omega^{\textbf{n}}_{r_{2}}|_{\partial\Omega}
in
L_{t}^{2}H^{m-\frac{3}{2}}(\partial\Omega).
Nevertheless, such a bound, due to the quantity
\partial_{\textbf{n}}\sigma
in the expression of
\omega^{\textbf{n}}_{r_{2}}
(see (
1.46
)), is unlikely to be uniform in
\kappa
(remember that we can only expect
\kappa^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\nabla^{2}\sigma\|_{L_{t}^{2}H_{co}^{m-2}}
to be uniformly controlled).
The way to circumvent this problem is to make further correction to the weight function
r_{2}.

More precisely, one can define

(1.51)

\displaystyle r_{0}=\frac{1}{R\beta(\theta)}\exp(\varepsilon R{\tilde{\sigma}}/C_{v}\gamma)\quad\text{ with }\,\tilde{\sigma}=\sigma-\varepsilon\mu(2\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2})\Gamma{\rm div\,}u

and switch to study
\omega_{r_{0}}^{\textbf{n}}=\colon\mathop{\rm curl}\nolimits(r_{0}u)\times\textbf{n},
whose boundary condition is changed into

(1.52)

\displaystyle\omega^{\textbf{n}}_{r_{0}}\big|_{\partial\Omega}=\frac{R\varepsilon}{C_{v}\gamma}r_{0}\partial_{\textbf{n}}\tilde{\sigma}\Pi u+2r_{0}\Pi\big(-au+(D\textbf{n})u\big)\big|_{\partial\Omega}.

Let us remark that the instant advantage is that
by the boundary condition (derived from
\eqref{NCNS-S2}_{2}
),

\displaystyle\partial_{\textbf{n}}\tilde{\sigma}=-\big(\frac{1}{R\beta}(\varepsilon\partial_{t}+\varepsilon u\cdot\nabla)u+\varepsilon\mu\lambda_{1}\Gamma\mathop{\rm curl}\nolimits\mathop{\rm curl}\nolimits u+\varepsilon\mu(2\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2})({\rm div\,}u)\nabla\Gamma\big)\cdot\textbf{n}\quad\text{ on }\quad{\partial\Omega},

one can control
|\partial_{\textbf{n}}\tilde{\sigma}|_{L_{t}^{2}H^{m-\frac{3}{2}}(\partial\Omega)}
uniformly in
(\varepsilon,\mu,\kappa).

The above considerations lead us to write
\omega_{r_{0}}^{\textbf{n}}\circ\Psi=\tilde{\zeta}_{1}+\tilde{\zeta}_{2},
where
\tilde{\zeta}_{1},\tilde{\zeta}_{2}
solve respectively the equation (
1.3.2
) (
1.3.2
) with
r_{2}
changed into
r_{0}.

Nevertheless, by the property (
15.30
), we find that to control
\tilde{\zeta}_{1}
in the space
L_{t}^{2}H_{co}^{m-1},
it requires the boundedness of

(\frac{1}{r_{0}})^{\Psi}|_{z=0}
in the space
L_{t}^{\infty}H^{m-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
which is missing.
To overcome this problem, we introduce further splittings:

\omega_{r_{0}}^{\textbf{n}}\circ\Psi={\zeta}_{3}+{\zeta}_{4}+{\zeta}_{5},
where

\zeta_{3}-\zeta_{5}
solves (
8.100
)-(
8.102
).
Note that the boundary condition is also carefully split for latter use. We comment also that
\zeta_{5}
solves the similar equation as
\tilde{\zeta}_{2}
with small variations.

To prove uniform estimates for
{\zeta}_{5},
there are still some (essential) troubles which force us to make the assumption (
1.19
) on
\mu
and
\kappa.
We will explain this point more carefully in the next subsection.

1.3.3.
Remarks on the assumption (
1.19
)

As mentioned in the last subsection,
the relation (
1.19
) between
\mu
and
\kappa
is made mainly to control the modified vorticity

\omega_{r_{0}}^{\textbf{n}}
or more precisely
\zeta_{5}
defined as the solution to the following (rough) system:

(1.53)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}(\partial_{t}+\tilde{u}\cdot\nabla){\zeta_{5}}-\mu\lambda_{1}\overline{\Gamma}(\frac{1}{r_{0}})^{\Psi}(\partial_{z}^{2}+\Delta_{{g}})\zeta_{5}=\big(\mathop{\rm curl}\nolimits G_{r_{0}}\times\textbf{n}\big)^{\Psi}+\cdots,\quad(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{3},\\[2.5pt]
\zeta_{5}|_{z=0}=0,\quad\zeta_{5}|_{t=0}=\zeta|_{t=0},\end{array}\right.

where

(1.54)

\displaystyle G_{r_{0}}=\colon u(\partial_{t}+u\cdot\nabla)r_{0}+\mu\lambda_{1}\overline{\Gamma}r_{0}^{-1}[\mathop{\rm curl}\nolimits\mathop{\rm curl}\nolimits,r_{0}]u.

Since
\zeta_{5}
vanishes on the boundary and the equation (
1.53
) does not involve any
singular terms, we expect to perform a direct energy estimate to control the
L_{t}^{\infty}H_{co}^{m-2}
norm of
\zeta_{5}.
By doing so, we arrive at the following energy estimate:

\displaystyle\|\zeta_{5}(t)\|_{H_{co}^{m-2}}^{2}+\mu\iota\|\nabla\zeta_{5}\|_{L_{t}^{2}H_{co}^{m-2}}^{2}\leq\|\zeta_{5}(0)\|_{H_{co}^{m-2}}^{2}

(1.55)

\displaystyle+\sum_{|I|\leq m-2}\bigg|\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{3}}Z^{I}(\mathop{\rm curl}\nolimits G_{r_{0}}\times\textbf{n})^{\Psi}\cdot Z^{I}\zeta_{5}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}s\bigg|+\bigg|\mu\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{3}}Z^{I}\big(\big(\frac{1}{r_{0}}\big)^{\Psi}\big)\partial_{z}\zeta_{5}\cdot\partial_{z}Z^{I}\zeta_{5}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}s\bigg|+\cdots

where
\cdots
denotes some quantities that are relatively easy to control and
\iota
stands for some constant independent of
\varepsilon,\mu,\kappa
. Let us comment that the interactions between the thermal boundary layer and the viscous boundary layer are mainly reflected in the first two terms in (
1.55
). Indeed,
we first observe that as (see (
7.15
))

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+u\cdot\nabla)r_{0}=-\frac{R\kappa}{C_{v}\gamma}r_{0}\Gamma{\rm div\,}(\beta\nabla\theta)+\cdots,

the term
\mathop{\rm curl}\nolimits G_{r_{0}}\times\textbf{n}
may involve three normal derivatives of
\theta
.
However, after some calculations, we can only expect
\kappa^{\frac{3}{2}}\nabla^{3}\theta
to be uniformly bounded in
L_{t}^{2}H_{co}^{m-2}.
The natural idea is to perform an integration by parts in space to obtain (see the definition of
G_{r_{0}}
in (
1.54
))

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{3}}Z^{I}(\mathop{\rm curl}\nolimits G_{r_{0}}\times\textbf{n})\cdot Z^{I}\zeta_{5}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}s\bigg|\lesssim\|\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla\zeta_{5}\|_{L_{t}^{2}H_{co}^{m-2}}\big(\kappa\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}+\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}\big)\|\nabla^{2}\theta\|_{L_{t}^{2}H_{co}^{m-2}}\Lambda\big(\frac{1}{c_{0}},{\mathcal{A}}_{m,t}\big)+\cdots

where
\Lambda
represents a polynomial function with respect to its arguments whose coefficients are independent of
(\varepsilon,\mu,\kappa)
. In view of the definition of

{\mathcal{E}}_{m,t}(\theta)
in (
1.23
), we have the control of
\kappa^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\nabla^{2}\theta\|_{L_{t}^{2}H_{co}^{m-2}}.
This
leads us to assume that:

\displaystyle\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim\kappa^{\frac{1}{2}},\,\kappa\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim\kappa^{\frac{1}{2}},\text{ or equivalently }\,\kappa\sim\mu.

As for the control of the second term in (
1.55
), the main problem we encounter is the estimate of

\displaystyle\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}\|Z^{I}\big(\big(\frac{1}{r_{0}}\big)^{\Psi}\big)\partial_{z}\zeta_{5}\|_{L_{t}^{2}L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{3})},\quad\text{ when }|I|=m-2.

Indeed,
due to the viscous boundary layer, the term

\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\partial_{z}\zeta_{5}\|_{L_{t}^{q}L^{P}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{3})}

is unlikely to be uniformly (in
\varepsilon,\mu
) bounded for any
2\leq q\leq+\infty,\,2<p\leq+\infty
(see Remark
8.15
for more explanations).
We are thus forced to attribute the
L_{t}^{\infty}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{3})
norm on
Z^{I}\big(\frac{1}{r_{0}}\big)^{\Psi}
which is not uniformly (in
\kappa
) bounded. As a matter of fact, by the Sobolev embedding and the definition (
1.23
) for
{\mathcal{E}}_{m,t}(\theta),
we have at best:

\displaystyle\kappa^{\frac{1}{2}}|\!|\!|\theta|\!|\!|_{m-2,\infty,t}\lesssim\kappa^{\frac{1}{2}}(\|\nabla\theta\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}H_{co}^{m-1}}+\|\theta\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}H_{co}^{m}})\lesssim{\mathcal{E}}_{m,t}(\theta).

To deal with this problem, we split again
\zeta_{5}
into two unknowns:
\zeta_{5}=\zeta_{51}+\zeta_{52},
and try to prove an estimate like:

(1.56)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{z}\zeta_{51}\|_{L_{t}^{2}L^{2}}+\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}|\!|\!|\partial_{z}\zeta_{52}|\!|\!|_{0,\infty,t}\lesssim\Lambda\big(\frac{1}{c_{0}},Y_{m}(0)\big)+\Lambda\big(\frac{1}{c_{0}},{\mathcal{N}}_{m,t}\big).

In this way, the term
\sum_{|I|=m-2}\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}\|Z^{I}\big(\big(\frac{1}{r_{0}}\big)^{\Psi}\big)\partial_{z}\zeta_{5}\|_{L_{t}^{2}L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{3})}
can be controlled as:

\displaystyle\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}|\!|\!|r_{0}^{-1}|\!|\!|_{m-2,\infty,t}\|\partial_{z}\zeta_{51}\|_{L_{t}^{2}L^{2}}+\|Z(r_{0}^{-1})\|_{L_{t}^{2}H_{co}^{m-3}}|\!|\!|\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{z}\zeta_{52}|\!|\!|_{0,\infty,t}.

In order to meet the requirement (
1.56
), we design carefully two systems (see (
8.2
) satisfied by
\zeta_{51},\zeta_{52}).
One of which is,
roughly speaking,

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\big(\partial_{t}-\mu\lambda_{1}\overline{\Gamma}b_{0}\partial_{z}^{2}\big)\zeta_{51}=\big(\mathop{\rm curl}\nolimits G_{r_{0}}\times\textbf{n}\big)^{\Psi}+\cdots,\\
\zeta_{51}|_{z=0}=0,\quad\zeta_{51}|_{t=0}=0.\end{array}\right.

Therefore, to prove the boundedness of
\|\partial_{z}\zeta_{51}\|_{L_{t}^{2}L^{2}},
one needs to control more or less
\|\mu^{-1}G_{r_{0}}\|_{L_{t}^{2}L^{2}}.

Nevertheless, in view of the definition (
1.54
),
we get after some computations that:

\displaystyle G_{r_{0}}=\lambda_{1}\overline{\Gamma}\big(\mu-\frac{\kappa}{C_{v}\gamma\lambda_{1}}\big)u\Delta\theta+\cdots

which, combined with the boundedness of

\kappa^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\Delta\theta\|_{L_{t}^{2}L^{2}},
leads us to assume the relation (
1.19
). At this stage, let us also mention that in order to prove
\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{z}\zeta_{52}
to be bounded in
L_{t,x}^{\infty},
we need to impose some compatibility condition on the data, which is satisfied once the initial velocity vanishes on the boundary. One can refer to
the proof of Lemma
8.13
for more details.

1.4.
Sketch of the uniform estimates

As the uniform regularity estimates are somehow delicate to establish, we outline in this subsection the main steps toward this goal.

\bullet

Step 1. Highest-order
\varepsilon
-dependent estimate
:

(\mu+\kappa)^{\frac{1}{2}}(\|(\varepsilon\sigma,\varepsilon u,\theta)\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}\underline{H}_{co}^{m}},\|(\varepsilon\nabla\sigma,\varepsilon{\rm div\,}u,\nabla\theta)\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}H_{co}^{m-1}}).

As the singular terms appear in the equations of
\sigma
and
u,
but not in the equation of
\theta,
we expect to get the highest-order estimates for
(\sigma,u)
with a weight
\varepsilon
and for
\theta
without weight. The parameter

(\mu+\kappa)^{\frac{1}{2}}
is also added in order to obtain
the estimates independent of
\mu,\kappa.
This is consistent with
[
2
]
when the physical domain is the whole space
\mathbb{R}^{3}.

\bullet

Step 2. Uniform estimates for time derivatives
:
\|(\sigma,u,\kappa\nabla\theta)\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}{\mathcal{H}}^{m-1,0}}.
Since the time derivatives commute with the singular terms, one expects to get the uniform estimates for time derivatives directly by energy estimates. However,
the
penalized operator
associated to singular terms in the system
(
1.17
)
is not skew-symmetric. Inspired by
[
2
]
,
we need to work on the unknown

w=u-\kappa\frac{\gamma-1}{\gamma}\Gamma(0)\beta(\theta)\nabla\theta
and perform energy estimates for
(\sigma,w,\kappa\nabla\theta).

\bullet

Step 3. Uniform estimates for
\theta.

Under suitable a priori knowledge on the properties of
u,
we can obtain some uniform estimates (in
\varepsilon,\mu
) for
\theta,
namely the quantities appearing in
{\mathcal{E}}_{m,t}(\theta)
(defined in (
1.23
)).
Such estimates will be useful in the following steps.

\bullet

Step 4 (Non)-uniform second normal derivatives.
In this step, we aim to control the quantities that involve the second normal derivatives of
(u,\sigma)
in the definition of
{\mathcal{E}}_{m,t}(\sigma,u)
(see (
1.22
)). Such estimates are achieved by working on
\omega_{\textbf{n}}=\mathop{\rm curl}\nolimits u\times\textbf{n}-2\Pi(-au+D\textbf{n}\cdot u)
which satisfies a convection-diffusion equation with vanishing boundary condition and on
\Delta\sigma
which solves a damped transport equation.

\bullet

Step 5. Uniform control for the compressible part-I
:

\kappa^{\frac{1}{2}}\|(\nabla\sigma,{\rm div\,}u)\|_{L_{t}^{2}H_{co}^{m-1}}.

In this step, we will use recursively the equation for
(\sigma,u)
to recover the higher order space conormal derivatives for the compressible part
(\nabla\sigma,{\rm div\,}u).

\bullet

Step 6. Uniform estimates for the incompressible part of the modified velocity
:
Denote
v=\mathbb{P}(r_{0}u)
the incompressible part of the velocity, where
\mathbb{P}
is the Leray projection defined in (
6.3
) and
r_{0}
is defined in (
1.51
). After lengthy but straightforward computations, one finds that
v
solves the equations:

(1.57)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}(\partial_{t}+u\cdot\nabla)v-\lambda_{1}\mu\frac{\overline{\Gamma}}{r_{0}}\Delta v+\nabla q=G-[\mathbb{P},u\cdot\nabla](r_{0}u)\quad\text{in}\quad{\Omega}\\[7.0pt]
v\cdot\textbf{n}=0,\quad\Pi(\partial_{\textbf{n}}v)=\Pi\big(-2ar_{0}u+(D\textbf{n})(\nabla\Psi+r_{0}u)\big)+\frac{R\varepsilon}{C_{v}\gamma}r_{0}\partial_{\textbf{n}}\tilde{\sigma}\Pi u\quad\text{on}\quad{\partial\Omega}\end{array}\right.

where
\nabla q=\colon\mathbb{Q}(G+\lambda_{1}\mu\frac{\overline{\Gamma}}{r_{0}}\Delta v)
and
G
is defined in (
7
).
We remark that in the derivation of
v,
we can not naively
consider the term
\mathbb{P}(u\cdot\nabla(r_{0}u))
as the source term, as it is not uniformly (in
\mu
) bounded in
L_{t}^{2}H_{co}^{m-1}.
However, we find that it is the case for the commutator
[\mathbb{P},u\cdot\nabla](r_{0}u),
see (
7.25
).

As no singular terms involved in the equation (
1.57
), one expects to perform direct energy estimates to obtain in the same time:

\|v\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}H_{co}^{m-1}},\quad\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\nabla v\|_{L_{t}^{2}H_{co}^{m-1}}.

Due to the appearance of the typical term like
\varepsilon\mu\nabla^{2}\sigma,
the source term
G
can not be bounded uniformly in

L_{t}^{2}H_{co}^{m-1}.
We will thus split the term
G
into two parts, one of which can be uniformly controlled in
L_{t}^{2}H_{co}^{m-1},
the other is bounded only in
L_{t}^{2}H_{co}^{m-2}
but can gain the extra weight
\varepsilon\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}
which is very useful to take benefits of the boundedness of
\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\nabla v\|_{L_{t}^{2}H_{co}^{m-1}}.

The pressure
\nabla q
is dealt with in the same fashion. We can thus close the estimates for
v.

\bullet

Step 7. Uniform estimates for the vorticity.
In this step, we aim to control
\|\nabla u\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}H_{co}^{m-2}}
or alternatively
\|\nabla(r_{0}u)\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}H_{co}^{m-2}}.

It is the most involved step and the main place where the relation (
1.19
) for
\mu
and
\kappa
is used.
As the difficulty lies in the estimate near the boundary, we will work on a local charts
\Omega_{i}.

By the virtue of the identities:

\partial_{\textbf{n}}(r_{0}u\cdot\textbf{n})={\rm div\,}(r_{0}u)-(\Pi\partial_{y^{1}}(r_{0}u))^{1}-(\Pi\partial_{y^{2}}(r_{0}u))^{2},\quad\Pi(\partial_{\textbf{n}}(r_{0}u))=\Pi(\mathop{\rm curl}\nolimits(r_{0}u)\times\textbf{n})-\Pi[(D\textbf{n})(r_{0}u)],

it suffices to control
\|\mathop{\rm curl}\nolimits(r_{0}u)\times\textbf{n}\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}H_{co}^{m-2}}.
We refer to the last subsection for the strategies of this estimate.

\bullet

Step 8. Control of
L_{t,x}^{\infty}
norm
{\mathcal{A}}_{m,t}(\theta).
We aim to control in this step the
L_{t,x}^{\infty}
norms appearing in
{\mathcal{A}}_{m,t}(\theta),
which will be useful in the next step. The most non-trivial part is the control of
|\!|\!|\nabla\theta|\!|\!|_{m-5,\infty,t},
or more precisely
|\!|\!|\partial_{\textbf{n}}\theta|\!|\!|_{m-5,\infty,t}.
To this end, we
take benefit of the Green function for the transport-diffusion equation (
9
) in half space with variable coefficients solved by
\partial_{\textbf{n}}\theta\circ\Psi,
where
\Psi
is the transformation associated to the normal geodesic coordinates
(
8.12
).

\bullet

Step 9. Uniform control for the compressible part-II
:

\|(\nabla\sigma,{\rm div\,}u)\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}H_{co}^{m-2}}.

In this step, we aim to get
L_{t}^{\infty}
type energy norms. Such an estimate is achieved again by iteration. Nevertheless, in this process, it requires the a-priori knowledge of
|\!|\!|u|\!|\!|_{[\frac{m}{2}]-1,\infty,t},
which is hopeful only after
{\rm div\,}u
is controlled in

L_{t}^{\infty}H_{co}^{[\frac{m}{2}]}.
Therefore, we will perform a two-tier iteration. That is, we first control
(\nabla\sigma,{\rm div\,}u)
in
L_{t}^{\infty}H_{co}^{m-4}
norm which only requires the
L_{t,x}^{\infty}
type norms for
\theta,\nabla\theta,
and then proceed to get the higher order estimates in
L_{t}^{\infty}H_{co}^{m-2}
by using the previous estimate.

\bullet

Step 10. Control of
{\mathcal{A}}_{m,t}(\sigma,u)

To finish the uniform estimates, it remains to control the
L_{t,x}^{\infty}
norms for
(\sigma,u)
defined in
{\mathcal{A}}_{m,t}(\sigma,u).
By the virtue
of the Sobolev embedding (
15.18
),
most of them can be bounded directly by previously controlled quantities. After some arguments, we reduce the matter to the control of
|\!|\!|(\mathop{\rm curl}\nolimits(r_{0}u)\times\textbf{n},\varepsilon\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}(\partial_{\textbf{n}}\omega_{\textbf{n}},\Delta\sigma))|\!|\!|_{m-5,\infty,t}
which bears some resemblance as the estimate of
|\!|\!|\nabla\theta|\!|\!|_{m-5,\infty,t}
done in Step 8.

Organization of the paper.

We carry out the estimates outlined in Step 1–Step 10 from Section 2 to Section 11. In Section 12, we summarize the estimates obtained in the previous sections to show
Proposition
1.10
. The proof of Theorem
1.1
is then presented in Section 13. In Section 14, we sketch the proof of the convergence result stated in Theorem
1.11
.
Finally, in appendix, we gather some useful estimates and prove some technical lemmas which are used throughout this work.

Further notations

\bullet
We denote
\Lambda(\cdot,\cdot)

a polynomial that may differ from line to line but independent of
\varepsilon\in(0,1],\mu,\kappa\in A.

\bullet
We use the notation
\lesssim
for

\leq C(1/c_{0})
for some number

C(1/c_{0})
that depends only on
1/c_{0}
(in particular, independent of
(\varepsilon,\mu,\kappa)).

\bullet
We use the notation
L_{t}^{p}L^{2}=L^{p}([0,t],L^{2}(\Omega)),\,(p=2,+\infty).

\bullet
We use frequently the lighten notation

{\mathcal{N}}_{m,T},\,{\mathcal{E}}_{m,T},

{\mathcal{A}}_{m,T}
and

Y_{m}(0)

for

{\mathcal{N}}_{m,T}^{\mu,\kappa}(\sigma^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon},\theta^{\varepsilon}),\,\,{\mathcal{E}}_{m,T}^{\mu,\kappa}(\sigma^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon},\theta^{\varepsilon}),\,\,{\mathcal{A}}_{m,T}^{\mu,\kappa}(\sigma^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon},\theta^{\varepsilon}),\,\,Y^{\varepsilon}_{m,\mu,\kappa}(0).

Caution of notations:

\bullet
As there are many parameters/quantities in the system (
1.17
), it is useful to keep in mind that

\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},R,C_{v},\gamma
are some physical constants while
\beta(\theta^{\varepsilon}),\Gamma(\varepsilon\sigma^{\varepsilon})
are some variable functions.

\bullet
For notational convenience, we skip the
\varepsilon-
dependence of the solutions when proving the a-priori estimates conducted in Section 2–Section 12.

2.
\varepsilon-
dependent highest order estimates.

In this section, we aim to prove some
\varepsilon-
dependent highest order energy estimates, which will be useful to obtain the desired uniform estimates afterwards. The main result in this section is the following:

Proposition 2.1
.

Assume that (
1.34
) is satisfied for some
T\in(0,1]
and
suppose
m\geq 7,

then for any
\varepsilon\in(0,1],(\mu,\kappa)\in A,
any
0<t\leq T,

the following estimate holds:

(2.1)

\displaystyle\kappa\|(\varepsilon\sigma,\varepsilon u,\theta)\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}\underline{H}_{co}^{m}}^{2}+\kappa\|(\nabla(\varepsilon\sigma,\theta),\varepsilon{\rm div\,}u)\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}H_{co}^{m-1}}^{2}

\displaystyle+\kappa\mu\big(\|\varepsilon\nabla u\|_{L_{t}^{2}\underline{H}_{co}^{m}}^{2}+\|\varepsilon\nabla{\rm div\,}u\|_{L_{t}^{2}H_{co}^{m-1}}^{2}\big)+\kappa^{2}\big(\|\nabla\theta\|_{L_{t}^{2}\underline{H}_{co}^{m}}^{2}+\|\nabla^{2}\theta\|_{L_{t}^{2}H_{co}^{m-1}}^{2}\big)

\displaystyle\lesssim Y_{m}^{2}(0)+(T^{\frac{1}{2}}+\varepsilon)\Lambda\big(\frac{1}{c_{0}},{\mathcal{A}}_{m,t}\big){\mathcal{E}}_{m,t}^{2}.

As the first attempt, one may expect to prove
the above proposition by performing
direct energy estimates on the system (
1.17
). Nevertheless, as (
1.17
) is not skew-symmetric, there shall be some difficulties when one
deals with the problematic terms
\frac{\gamma-1}{\gamma}\kappa\Gamma{\rm div\,}(\beta\nabla\theta)
(in the equation for
\sigma
) and
{\rm div\,}u
(in the equation for
\theta
). Therefore,
as in
[
2
]
, we introduce the new unknown

(2.2)

\varrho=\frac{\varepsilon}{\gamma}\sigma-\frac{(\gamma-1)C_{v}}{\gamma R}\theta,

which plays the same role as
\varepsilon\rho.

Denote
u_{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon u
for simplicity, we find after some computations that
(\varrho,u_{\varepsilon},\theta)
solves the equations which is amenable to the energy estimates:

(2.3)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}(\partial_{t}+u\cdot\nabla)\varrho+\frac{1}{\gamma\varepsilon}{\rm div\,}u_{\varepsilon}=0,\\[5.0pt]
\frac{1}{R\beta(\theta)}(\partial_{t}+u\cdot\nabla)u_{\varepsilon}+\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon}\nabla\varrho+\frac{(\gamma-1)C_{v}}{R\varepsilon}\nabla\theta-\mu{\rm div\,}{\mathcal{L}}u_{\varepsilon}=0,\\[5.0pt]
\frac{C_{v}}{R}(\partial_{t}+u\cdot\nabla)\theta+\frac{{\rm div\,}u_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon}-\Gamma(\varepsilon\sigma){\rm div\,}(\beta\nabla\theta)=\mu\varepsilon^{2}\Gamma(\varepsilon\sigma){\mathcal{L}}u\cdot\mathbb{S}u.\end{array}\right.

We shall prove Proposition
2.1
by showing Propositions
2.2
and
2.4
which are presented respectively in the following two subsections.

2.1.
\varepsilon-
dependent estimates-I

Proposition 2.2
.

Under the same assumption as in Proposition
2.1
, for any
\varepsilon\in(0,1],(\mu,\kappa)\in A,
any
0<t\leq T,
it holds that:

(2.4)

\displaystyle\kappa\|(\varrho,u_{\varepsilon},\theta)\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}\underline{H}_{co}^{m}}^{2}+\kappa\mu\|\nabla u_{\varepsilon}\|_{L_{t}^{2}\underline{H}_{co}^{m}}^{2}+\kappa^{2}\|\nabla\theta\|_{L_{t}^{2}\underline{H}_{co}^{m}}^{2}\lesssim Y^{2}_{m}(0)+(T^{\frac{1}{2}}+\varepsilon)\Lambda\big(\frac{1}{c_{0}},{\mathcal{A}}_{m,t}\big){\mathcal{E}}_{m,t}^{2}.

Proof.

Applying the vector field
Z^{I},(|I|\leq m-1,
or
Z^{I}=Z_{j}Z^{\tilde{I}},|\tilde{I}|\leq m-1,j=1,2\cdots M
) on the system (
2.3
) and denoting
(\varrho^{I},u_{\varepsilon}^{I},\theta^{I})=Z^{I}(\varrho,u_{\varepsilon},\theta),
we find the following system:

(2.5)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}(\partial_{t}+u\cdot\nabla)\varrho^{I}+\frac{1}{\gamma\varepsilon}{\rm div\,}u_{\varepsilon}^{I}={\mathcal{C}}_{\varrho}^{I},\\[5.0pt]
\frac{1}{R\beta(\theta)}(\partial_{t}+u\cdot\nabla)u_{\varepsilon}^{I}+\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon}\nabla\varrho^{I}+\frac{(\gamma-1)C_{v}}{R\varepsilon}\nabla\theta^{I}-\mu\Gamma(\lambda_{1}{\rm div\,}Z^{I}\nabla u_{\varepsilon}+(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2})\nabla Z^{I}{\rm div\,}u_{\varepsilon})={\mathcal{C}}_{u_{\varepsilon}}^{I},\\[5.0pt]
\frac{C_{v}}{R}(\partial_{t}+u\cdot\nabla)\theta^{I}+\frac{{\rm div\,}u_{\varepsilon}^{I}}{\varepsilon}-\Gamma(\varepsilon\sigma){\rm div\,}(\beta Z^{I}\nabla\theta)={\mathcal{C}}_{\theta}^{I},\end{array}\right.

where
({\mathcal{C}}_{\varrho}^{I},{\mathcal{C}}_{u_{\varepsilon}}^{I},{\mathcal{C}}_{\theta}^{I})
are defined as:

(2.6)

{\mathcal{C}}_{\varrho}^{I}=-[Z^{I},u\cdot\nabla]\varrho-\frac{1}{\gamma}[Z^{I},{\rm div\,}]u,

(2.7)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}_{u_{\varepsilon}}^{I}=

\displaystyle\underbrace{-\frac{1}{R\beta(\theta)}[Z^{I},u\cdot\nabla]u_{\varepsilon}}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{u_{\varepsilon,1}}^{I}}\underbrace{-\frac{1}{\beta(\theta)}[Z^{I},\beta(\theta)\nabla]\sigma+\frac{\mu}{\beta(\theta)}[Z^{I},\beta\Gamma]{\rm div\,}{\mathcal{L}}u_{\varepsilon}}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{u_{\varepsilon,2}}^{I}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\mu\Gamma\big(\lambda_{1}[Z^{I},{\rm div\,}]\nabla u_{\varepsilon}+(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2})[Z^{I},\nabla]{\rm div\,}u_{\varepsilon}\big)}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{u_{\varepsilon,3}}^{I}},

(2.8)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}_{\theta}^{I}=

\displaystyle-\frac{C_{v}}{R}[Z^{I},u\cdot\nabla]\theta-[Z^{I},{\rm div\,}]u+\mu\varepsilon^{2}Z^{I}(\Gamma{\mathcal{L}}u\cdot\mathbb{S}u)
